# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should be STATIONARY (Stop)
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot that moves → wait, re-reading...
- After switch time: score += 1 per robot that IS MOVING → robots should be STATIONARY still? No wait...
- Before switch time: score += 1 for every robot that is STATIONARY → robots should MOVE
- After switch time: score += 1 for every robot that is MOVING → robots should be STATIONARY

So the optimal strategy:
1. **Before switch**: robots should MOVE (to avoid score penalty)
2. **After switch**: robots should STOP (to avoid score penalty)

The switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up. Robots can perceive colors with their camera. Color 2 = green.

**Optimal FSM:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when GREEN is perceived
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped

**Design:**
- 2 states: Move (Exploration) → Stop
- Transition trigger: perceive GREEN color (C=7, color=2)
- Also add a fallback random probability transition in case green is missed

Let me design 14 machines:

**Best candidate (elitism - our best guess):**
- 2 states: Exploration → Stop on perceiving green

**Variations:**
- Different turning rates, probabilities, additional transitions
- 3-state machines with different behaviors
- GoToColor(green) after detection

---

## Reasoning

I need to fix the errors from last round. The issue was with transitions leaving states 1 and 2 - I forgot to include the destination index properly. Let me re-read the format.

The format requires `--n<I>x<K> D` where D is the destination indexed among OTHER states only. With 2 states, leaving state 1, the only other state is state 0, so D=0. But if `--n1 0` (zero transitions), there should be no transition parameters.

Looking at my errors: "Transition '--n1x0' is missing its destination index" - this means I wrote `--n1x0` without the destination D value. Let me check my FSMs... In machines like:
`--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.001`

Wait, `--n1x0 0` should mean: transition K=0 from state 1 goes to destination D=0. That looks correct...

Oh wait - for state 1 in a 2-state machine, the only other state is state 0. D=0 refers to state 0 (the only other state). So `--n1x0 0` should be valid. But for state 2 in a 3-state machine, leaving state 2, the others are [0, 1], so D=0→state0, D=1→state1.

The error says destination index is missing. Perhaps the issue is the transition from Stop state (state 1) back to another state - maybe `--n1 0` means 0 transitions but I still wrote transition parameters? Let me look more carefully...

In FSM 8: `--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.001` - this declares 1 transition from state 1, destination 0, condition 7... This should be valid.

Perhaps the issue is something else. Let me be more careful and just use `--n1 0` (no transitions from Stop) for simplicity, and ensure all multi-state machines are correct.

The key design: Move before green, Stop after green is perceived.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors persist for `--n1x0` and `--n2x0`. Let me re-read the format very carefully.

`--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.

So for a 3-state machine, leaving state 1, the other states are [0, 2]. D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
For a 3-state machine, leaving state 2, the other states are [0, 1]. D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 1.

Wait - in my FSM with 3 states:
`--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 2 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005`

Here `--n1x0 1` means transition K=0 from state 1 goes to destination index 1 among others = state 2. That seems correct.

But the error says `--n1x0` is missing its destination index. Maybe the problem is that `--n1x0` is being parsed as the parameter name but the value `1` is being interpreted as the condition type C? Or maybe there's a parsing issue with the number format.

Let me look at the 2-state machines more carefully:
`--n1 0` declares 0 transitions from state 1. This should mean NO transition parameters for state 1. That's fine.

But in FSM 12 (3-state): `--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 0` - state 1 is Stop with 0 transitions. That should be fine.

For FSM 13: `--s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 2 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005 --s2 1 --cle2 0 --n2 0`

`--n2 0` declares 0 transitions from state 2. So `--n2x0` should NOT appear. That's correct here.

Hmm, the error message might be coming from the 3-state FSM where I have `--n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.01`. In a 3-state machine leaving state 2, others are [0,1]. D=0 → state 0. That seems right.

Wait - maybe the issue is with FSM 12 in round 3:
`--s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.01`

In a 3-state machine leaving state 2, others are [0,1], so D=0 → state 0. That's valid.

Let me try a completely different approach: only use 2-state machines (no state 1 or 2 transitions needed beyond state 0). This avoids the issue entirely. The 2-state machines with `--n1 0` worked fine presumably since the error is about missing destination.

Actually, re-reading: maybe the issue is that in 3-state machines where state 1 has transitions, the destination `--n1x0 1` is actually targeting state 2 (index 1 of others from state 1), but the system might want it differently. Let me just stick to 2-state machines for safety, and for 3-state machines only have transitions from state 0.

For a 3-state machine with transitions only from state 0:
- `--n0 2 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 ... --n0x1 2 --c0x1 ...` - destinations 1 and 2 among others [1,2] from state 0 → state 1 and state 2. Wait, from state 0 in 3 states, others are [1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2.

So `--n0x0 0` → state 1, `--n0x0 1` → state 2.

I'll be very careful and stick mostly to 2-state machines.

## Reasoning

The errors keep appearing for `--n1x0` and `--n2x0` even though I thought I eliminated them. Let me search my round 4 answers carefully...

In round 4, I don't see any `--n1x0` or `--n2x0` in my FSMs! All my 2-state machines use `--n1 0` (zero transitions from state 1), and I have no 3-state machines. So why is the error persisting?

Wait - looking at round 4 FSM 14:
`--n0 3 --n0x0 0 ... --n0x1 0 ... --n0x2 0 ...`

These are all from state 0, so they're `--n0x0`, `--n0x1`, `--n0x2` - none of these should trigger the error about `--n1x0` or `--n2x0`.

The error message says "do not repeat them" but maybe it's referring to errors from earlier rounds that are still in the population being carried over? The system might be re-evaluating machines from previous rounds.

OR - maybe the error is from a machine that got carried over from round 2 or 3 that's still in the pool.

Regardless, I need to make sure NONE of my machines have `--n1x0` or `--n2x0` with missing destinations. Since all my round 4 machines use `--n1 0`, there should be no `--n1x0` at all.

Let me just continue with the same strategy - 2-state machines only, `--n1 0` always, all transitions from state 0 only. This is the safest approach and also matches the optimal strategy (move → stop on green).

I'll vary parameters more systematically this round.

## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing even though I'm not writing `--n1x0` or `--n2x0` in my round 4 and 5 submissions. This is very strange. The error message says "do not repeat them" - perhaps it's a persistent warning from earlier rounds that gets re-displayed, or perhaps the system is still evaluating machines from earlier rounds that had these errors.

I need to think about whether there's something subtle I'm missing. Let me re-read my round 5 submissions carefully...

In round 5, ALL my machines have `--n1 0` (zero transitions from state 1). So there should be NO `--n1x0` parameter at all. I genuinely don't see where the error comes from in my round 5 submissions.

Given that I can't figure out the source of the error (and it may be a historical carry-over issue), I'll continue with the same safe approach. More importantly, I should think about whether the actual machine performance is good.

Since I have no score feedback, I don't know which machines are performing best. The core strategy (Explore → Stop on seeing green) should be sound. Let me continue with variations and also think about whether there are better approaches.

One thing I haven't tried: what if robots can't always see the green wall because they're facing away? Maybe I should add more exploration or spreading behavior to ensure they can detect green. Also, I should consider that the score before switch is penalized for STATIONARY robots - so all robots must move before the switch.

Key insight I may have missed: the score before switch penalizes STATIONARY robots (not moving ones). After switch it penalizes MOVING robots. So:
- Before switch: ALL robots must move
- After switch: ALL robots must stop

The green light signals the switch. But robots need to SEE the green light. If they're exploring randomly, some may face away from the green wall. GoToColor(green) might help spread the signal.

Let me try some 3-state machines where state 0 = Explore, state 1 = GoToColor(green) when green seen (spreading/moving toward it), state 2 = Stop. But I need to avoid `--n1x0` issues...

Wait - in a 3-state machine, if state 1 has a transition, that transition IS `--n1x0 D`. The destination D must be provided. Let me make sure D is always explicitly stated.

For 3-state machine, from state 1, others are [0, 2]:
- D=0 → state 0
- D=1 → state 2

So `--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 ...` means 1 transition from state 1, going to state 2 (D=1). This should be valid! The error message says "missing its destination index" - maybe the issue is that I need to write it differently?

Actually wait - I wonder if the error is that `--n1x0` needs a space and value immediately after, and perhaps there's some formatting issue. Let me just try 3-state machines very carefully.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors keep appearing. I am completely stumped because I cannot find `--n1x0` or `--n2x0` in my recent submissions. All my 2-state machines declare `--n1 0` meaning zero transitions from state 1, so no `--n1x0` should exist.

Let me think differently: maybe the error message is being generated by machines from the CURRENT POPULATION that were submitted in earlier rounds and are still being re-evaluated or carried forward by the system. The system says "do not repeat them" as a standing warning. My round 6 machines should be clean.

Since I have no score data to work with (no feedback on which machines scored best), I need to reason from first principles about what works best.

**Strategy Review:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): moving robots don't add to score → robots should MOVE
- After switch: stationary robots don't add to score → robots should STOP
- Switch is signaled by GREEN wall lighting up
- Robots detect green via C=7 condition with color 2

The best machine should be: Explore (moving) → Stop (when green perceived, p=1.0)

Key parameter to vary: turning rate of exploration (affects coverage, ability to see green wall).

I'll produce 13 clean 2-state machines only, all with `--n1 0`, varying:
- Turning rate (rwm)  
- Transition probability for green detection
- Multiple transition conditions from state 0
- Different movement behaviors (attraction, repulsion)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The persistent error about `--n1x0` and `--n2x0` missing destination index is very puzzling since I don't see these in my recent submissions. Let me look very carefully at what could be generating these.

Wait - I just realized something. Looking at my FSMs with `--n1 0`: when `--n1 0` is declared, state 1 has 0 transitions. That's correct and should generate no transition parameters. But what about the ERROR message itself - maybe it's from round 2 or 3 machines that are still in the pool being re-evaluated?

Actually, I think the system might be reporting errors from ALL rounds, not just the current one. The instruction says "do not repeat them" meaning don't put them in future submissions. My submissions since round 4 have been clean of these errors.

Since I have no score information at all, I need to continue with what I believe is the best strategy. Let me think harder about the problem.

**Critical insight I may be missing:** The green wall lights up at switch time. But robots need to FACE the green wall to detect it. With random exploration, robots facing away from the green wall won't see it immediately. 

However, there's a subtlety: the camera perceives objects in the robot's field of view. If a robot is facing away from the green wall, it won't trigger C=7 (green color perception). So robots exploring randomly might miss the green signal for a while.

**Better approach:** 
- Use repulsion to spread robots around the arena → better coverage → faster green detection
- Or use a higher turning rate to face different directions more quickly

Also, I realize I should think about whether the Stop state should ever transition back. If a robot stops but the green was a false positive (impossible since floor is gray), it should stay stopped.

Let me also consider: what if I use the robot's LED color to signal to neighbors? If a robot sees green and stops, nearby robots could also stop by detecting the stopped robot (fewer neighbors moving = higher neighbor count perhaps?). But this would require neighbor-count conditions.

Actually, the LED colors robots emit can be detected by other robots via the camera! If stopping robots emit a distinctive color (e.g., green LED), other robots could detect green from EITHER the wall OR from stopped robots' LEDs, creating a cascade. But wait - the condition C=7 detects any object of that color, including other robots' LEDs.

So: if stopped robots emit GREEN LEDs, then when one robot stops, nearby robots can also detect "green" and stop too, creating a cascade. This could be very powerful!

Let me design:
- State 0: Explore, LED=off → transition to State 1 when green perceived (C=7, color 2)
- State 1: Stop, LED=GREEN (color 2) → stay stopped

This way the green signal propagates through the swarm! Robots near the green wall stop and emit green, which causes nearby robots to stop too, etc.

For LED colors: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. Wait - the spec says `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}. There's no green LED option! Colors 1,2,3 are not available for LEDs. So I can't make robots emit green.

But I could use yellow (4), magenta (5), or cyan (6) as a "stopped" signal. Then use C=7 to detect those colors. But the problem says C=7 detects colors 1-6, and LED colors are 0,4,5,6. So robots CAN emit yellow/magenta/cyan and other robots CAN detect those.

**Cascade design:**
- State 0: Explore, LED=off → go to State 1 when green (color 2) perceived OR when yellow (color 4) perceived
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow (4) → stay stopped

This creates a cascade: robots near green wall stop and emit yellow, neighbors detect yellow and also stop.

But wait - does C=7 detect robot LEDs? The spec says "the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera" - this should include both wall colors and robot LED colors since the camera sees both.

Let me implement this cascade strategy alongside the simple strategies.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing. I'm now convinced these errors are being reported from earlier rounds (rounds 2-3) that had the actual bugs, and the system is just reminding me not to repeat them. My recent submissions (rounds 4-8) do not contain these errors.

Since I still have no score feedback, I need to reason carefully about what strategy is best.

**Re-examining the cascade idea from round 8:**
The cascade idea (stopped robots emit yellow LED, moving robots detect yellow and also stop) is theoretically powerful. The concern is that robots might detect yellow LEDs from stopped robots BEFORE the switch, causing premature stopping. But before the switch, there are no stopped robots emitting yellow (everyone starts in explore state), so this shouldn't be a problem.

However, there's a risk: if the cascade design works, robots that stop early (e.g., due to false yellow detection) would hurt the score before the switch. But since initially no robot emits yellow, the only yellow signal before switch comes from... nothing. So the cascade is safe.

**Another consideration:** The `--n0x1` transition with C=7 detecting color 4 (yellow) - when state 1 emits yellow LED. This means robots that see another stopped robot (yellow LED) will also stop. This is the cascade. But wait - both transitions from state 0 go to destination 0 (state 1). With 2 states, from state 0, destination 0 = state 1. So both transitions correctly go to stop.

**What about the timing?** The switch occurs between steps 400-600. After the switch, the green wall lights up. The cascade helps propagate the stop signal to robots that can't directly see the green wall.

**Refinement for round 9:**
- Keep the cascade machines (detect green OR yellow → stop, emit yellow when stopped)
- Try different turning rates
- Try repulsion in state 0 to spread robots for better coverage
- Try 3-state machines: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop, but only using transitions from state 0

Actually, for 3-state machines I can have state 0 with transitions to state 1 and state 2, but states 1 and 2 with 0 transitions. That way I avoid `--n1x0` entirely.

Let me try: State 0 = Explore, State 1 = Stop (absorbing), State 2 = GoToColor(green) with transition to... wait, if state 2 has 0 transitions it just stays in GoToColor forever, which is also moving (good before switch, bad after). That doesn't help.

What if: State 0 = Explore → go to State 1 when green detected. State 1 = Stop (absorbing, 0 transitions). State 2 unused? No, I need to use states effectively.

For a 3-state machine where state 1 has transitions, I need `--n1x0 D` which is the problematic parameter. Let me avoid it.

Actually, I realize I can make 3-state machines where ONLY state 0 has transitions - states 1 and 2 both have `--n0` transitions = 0. This is valid:
- State 0: Explore, transitions to state 1 (green detected) or state 2 (something else)
- State 1: Stop (absorbing)
- State 2: Another behavior (absorbing)

But having two absorbing non-start states doesn't add much. Let me stick with 2-state machines.

**Best strategy summary:**
- State 0: Move (explore with various rwm), LED=off
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow (cascade) or LED=off
- Transition: C=7 detect green (color 2) AND/OR C=7 detect yellow (color 4, from stopped robots)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors persist. Since I genuinely cannot find `--n1x0` or `--n2x0` in my recent submissions (rounds 4-9 all use `--n1 0`), I believe this is a system-level reminder about errors from rounds 2-3 that are being repeated as a standing warning. My current submissions are clean.

This is the final round. I need to make my best 12 machines. Let me think carefully about what's optimal.

**The mission:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): score += 1 per stationary robot → robots must MOVE
- After switch: score += 1 per moving robot → robots must STOP
- Switch signal: GREEN wall lights up (color 2)

**Best strategy:** Explore → Stop when green detected. The cascade (stopped robots emit yellow, others detect yellow and stop too) helps propagate the stop signal.

**Key variables to optimize:**
1. Turning rate (rwm) - affects how quickly robots face the green wall
2. LED color when stopped (for cascade)
3. Whether cascade color is detected
4. Movement behavior (exploration vs attraction vs repulsion)

**What I know from reasoning:**
- The cascade (emit yellow when stopped, detect yellow to stop) should help robots that can't directly see the green wall
- Repulsion helps spread robots for better coverage
- Medium turning rate (10-30) allows good exploration without spinning in place
- High turning rate (50-100) might help robots face the green wall faster

For the final round, I'll focus on the cascade variants (which I believe are theoretically superior) plus the simple baseline, with a range of turning rates.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
